A `drain`-kind write whose loaded scope equals its own `replaced` (the initial scope, epoch 0, before any change) is a refresh (withhold paragraph below) and is counted in neither drain counter. `writeUpdateBody` is the one point both receipt routes reach (round 6, I-1): a direct receipt write through `sendUpdateCounted`, which sets `sentMeta["replay"]` before it writes, and a receipt queued behind forwards (`sendBatchUpdate` -> `queueBehindForwards` when `withdrawBehindForwards()` holds, the normal state of a busy peer), which the forward worker writes through `writeUpdateGated(update, item.originated)` after `session.sentMeta = item.meta` (`{"replay": <kind>}`), never through `sendUpdateCounted`. `sendUpdateCounted`'s `counts` delta is not the counter, because the queued route never reaches it. The figure counts what the writer admitted under its rule "Named commits must not count paths withheld by the session as sent". Re-advertised is counted from the admitted NLRI, not at the verdict, because three refusals can stop a path: the RFC 4271 Section 5.1.3 next-hop withhold in `writeUpdateGated` (`return nil` after the egress filter, before the writer runs), `allow`'s refusal of an `adjOutExpected` key whose revision moved or whose table `beginAdjOut` reset for a new session, and the owed-key refusal of the rs spec; a path refused by the first two is counted in none of the three, and a path refused at an owed key is counted once, in the delta of the rs spec's per-`Peer` owed-refusal counter (count 3 below), and in neither drain counter (round 5, I-5; rs round 7, N-5). Each count is taken once per write, never once per filter pass (rs round 7, I-1): `writeUpdateBody` runs `ownership.filter` a second time when the first pass removed NLRI (`ownership.removed != 0`), but `ownership.announcedRoutes` is incremented only in `adjOutWrite.record` (`recordSection`, `session_ownership.go`), which runs once, after the final pass, so the re-advertised addition beside `updateSendTotals.announcedRoutes` adds each admitted NLRI once; the withheld count is taken at the verdict, once per write, before `ownership.filter` runs; the owed-refusal counter's single application per write is the rs spec's fix (effects staged on `adjOutWrite` during `filter`, reset between the two passes, and applied once, under `writeMu` with the table mutex, after the last pass that ran: at the `length == 0` return, at the PATHS-LIMIT `n == 0` return, and on the write path before `record`), which this spec relies on and does not restate.
        2. Withheld: the NLRI of drain-marked replay bodies the writer withholds under the withhold rule below, counted at the verdict in the peer's withheld drain counter, by the same section walk it keys provenance with (round 5, I-1). The two drain counters, re-advertised and withheld, are per-peer reactor counters, both written under `writeMu`.
        3. Receipt replays refused at an owed key during the drain: the delta, per peer, of the owed-refusal counter the rs spec (`plan/immediate/spec-bgp-rs-replacement-on-withdrawal.md`) defines, read once when the handler sets the drain flag and once at the drain reply. Under the rs spec's round 6 (B-1, option (i)) that counter is one per-peer monotonic atomic on the destination's `Peer` (`reactor/peer.go`), for which `adjOutWrite` (`reactor/session_ownership.go`) stages during `filter` one count per NLRI `allow` refuses with the owed-refused outcome, and to which `writeUpdateBody` (`reactor/session_write.go`) adds the staged count once per write, under `writeMu` with the table mutex (rs round 7, I-1: staged, reset between the two filter passes, applied after the last pass; round 8, N-1), on whichever goroutine writes (so a receipt queued behind forwards is counted when the forward worker writes it), never reset, and read only as a delta; this spec names it as the rs spec does and does not define it. The counter counts every `adjOutExpected` refusal at an owed key whatever replay carried it, so the delta also counts a ROUTE-REFRESH or `clear bgp rib out` refusal inside the drain window, which is why the line names the figure "receipt replays refused at an owed key during the drain" and not "drain replays refused" (round 7, item (5)). A refusal that happens after the reply read, of a write still queued then, is outside the figure, as it is outside the other two counts. The delta covers the drain replay, because it is an `adjOutExpected` write through `allow`, so no bgp-rib figure is needed for it: the update-route reply carries no owed-refusal field, and the `refused` of the `request bgp rib drain-replay` answer keeps its HEAD meaning (command errors, `resendRoutesWithCursor`), which the handler does not report as this count. Before the rs spec lands no key is owed and the counter does not exist, so the line reports the figure as absent, not as 0 (round 5, I-6; rs round 6).
        4. Sent untagged (no room): the announced NLRI the writer sent without f because f left no room for them in any message: an NLRI too large for the space f leaves, written alone; every NLRI of a section whose attributes alone exceed the maximum with f, written alone; or the NLRI of a remainder the splitter cannot frame when f(remainder) does not fit (the split refusal, Transformation Path, drain verdict; redesign round 3, I-2), live writes and replays alike, counted in `Session.writeUpdateBody` under `writeMu` in the peer's sent-untagged drain counter, a per-peer reactor counter beside the other two. A `drain`-kind write counted here is not counted re-advertised, so every drain replay the writer admits is counted once, in re-advertised or here, and none falls outside the reply (redesign round 2, N-1). The figure is printed as "sent untagged (no room)", and the warning line names each such write.
        The handler reads all four at the drain reply, when the wait ends (round 3, N-8; round 5, I-5, I-6).
   - The drain verdict is ONE reactor function, declared once, whose inputs are the path's provenance and the destination and whose answer is untouched, tag, or tag with LOCAL_PREF 0:

     | Destination | Originated by Ze | Learned from an eBGP peer | Learned from an iBGP peer |
     |-------------|------------------|---------------------------|---------------------------|
     | eBGP peer, not a route-server client | tag | tag | tag |
     | eBGP route-server client (`PeerFilterInfo.RSClient`) | tag | untouched (RFC 7947 Section 2.2.4) | untouched (RFC 7947 Section 2.2.4) |
     | iBGP peer, client or not | tag, LOCAL_PREF 0 | tag, LOCAL_PREF 0 (substitutes Section 4.2's inbound policy, Q-10) | untouched: reflection, outside RFC 8326's scope; LOCAL_PREF kept (RFC 4456 Section 10) |

     The verdict keys on provenance and destination, not on the next hop (round 2, I-1, rejected with evidence in Required Reading): a path to an eBGP peer with `next-hop unchanged` is tagged, because Section 4.2 tags "the paths propagated over the session"; a reflected path with `next-hop self` stays untagged, because tagging an iBGP copy means LOCAL_PREF 0, which RFC 4456 Section 10 says a reflector SHOULD NOT set (Known Limitations).
   - Send-community, under Q-11 (a) (round 4, I-5): the verdict also reads whether the destination suppresses standard communities (`sendCommunitySuppression` over `PeerSettings.SendCommunity`, the reader both existing mask paths use). Where it does, the answer drops the tag: a "tag" cell becomes untouched (so a drain replay is withheld and counted withheld, and the peer is named once in the log: "bgp drain: peer <name> does not send standard communities; GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN not sent"), and a "tag, LOCAL_PREF 0" cell becomes "LOCAL_PREF 0". The forward rails then need no change to their `applyFactsSendCommunity` order, and the gate, which applies no mask today, does not send the tag against the setting.
   - The verdict runs once per write. Its inputs are a provenance value (originated, learned from eBGP, learned from iBGP, or none), the destination's class (eBGP, eBGP route-server client, iBGP), taken from `PeerSettings.IsIBGP` and `RSClient`, and the destination's standard-community mask; it never derives iBGP-ness from `PeerFilterInfo` AS numbers, which disagree with `IsIBGP` under a migration AS (round 3, N-1). Provenance none is untagged with a warning naming the peer and the path, never classed as an origination or as eBGP-learned. The verdict and its rewrite f run at ONE site, `Session.writeUpdateBody` (`session_write.go`), the funnel every UPDATE body reaches (verified 2026-10-14 at the producers: `writeRawUpdateBody`, `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody`, `writeUpdateWithoutAIGP` and `writeOriginatedRawUpdate` all end there, and `Session.writeMessageWithin` and `SendRawMessage` route an UPDATE to `writeOriginatedRawUpdate`; the other socket writes in `reactor/` carry no UPDATE). The site is after the body is copied into `writeBuf` and `clearTransmittedLabelIndex` has run, inside the ownership section `beginAdjOut` opened, and before `ownership.filter`, so the adj-out signatures `ownership.record` takes stay those of the wire (redesign 2026-10-14: the drain step leaves `r.orderedEgressSteps`, the route-server rail and `exportFilterForBody`, and is not kept there beside the writer, `ai/rules/no-layering.md`). The writer receives U, the undrained egress body: export policy, next-hop normalization and the rail's send-community mask already applied. It loads the scope once per write, at entry to `writeUpdateBody`, after the AIGP branch (`writeUpdateWithoutAIGP` re-enters and loads there) and before `beginAdjOut`, still inside the caller's `writeMu`; that one value decides both the near-maximum split (below) and f. When the cell is untouched it writes U as today, so the undrained cost is that one atomic load. When the cell changes the body, the writer copies U from `writeBuf` into a pooled buffer (`getReadBuf`, the handle `writeUpdateWithoutAIGP` takes for the AIGP strip) and writes f(U, scope) from it back into `writeBuf`: every attribute copied as `stripAIGPAttributes` copies it, COMMUNITIES gaining 65535:0 only when U's COMMUNITIES lacks it (a COMMUNITIES attribute created in type-code order when U has none), and LOCAL_PREF set to 0 for a "tag, LOCAL_PREF 0" or "LOCAL_PREF 0" cell (the attribute created when U has none). One pool buffer and two copies, no allocation: the AIGP strip pattern (`ai/rules/performance.md`). U stays in that buffer until the sent-event call carries its attribute block (sent event, below); the buffer returns to its pool when `writeUpdateBody` returns. The near-maximum split (A-19; redesign round 1, I-1; redesign round 2, I-1, I-2) runs only when this write's f(U) would exceed the destination's maximum, and the writer computes f's growth g for this write exactly, from the cell and U: 0 for an untouched cell, for a tag cell whose U already carries 65535:0, and for a LOCAL_PREF that U already carries (f replaces the 4-octet value in place, same size); 4 for a value added to an existing COMMUNITIES, or 5 when that attribute's length crosses 255 and it takes the extended-length second length octet; 7 for a COMMUNITIES attribute created (3-octet header, one 4-octet value); 7 for a LOCAL_PREF attribute created (3-octet header, 4-octet value). g is the sum of the two parts the cell applies, at most 14 (redesign round 1, N-1). The cell needs the provenance, which for a receipt replay is the adj-out owner the writer reads inside the ownership section (A-18), so the decision is taken there: after the verdict and the withhold (a withheld replay writes nothing and is never split), before `ownership.filter`. When g is above 0 and the header plus U plus g exceeds the destination's maximum (`len(s.writeBuf.Buffer())`), the write ends its ownership section with `release`, nothing filtered, recorded, counted or written, which leaves the table as the existing return for a write the filter admits nothing from leaves it (`session_write.go`, `session_ownership.go`), and only then splits: `beginAdjOut` holds `s.adjOut.mu` until `release`, so a chunk that re-entered `writeUpdateBody` from inside that section would wait on the lock its own write holds. At that point U is in `writeBuf` (the writer copied it there, and a `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody` chunk already arrives there), every chunk's own write copies itself into `writeBuf[HeaderLen:]` and f writes back there, and `Splitter.Split` reads U again for each next chunk (`splitUpdateIPv4` passes `u.PathAttributes` through unchanged and slices its NLRI chunks from `u.NLRI`; the MP path reads `mpReachInfo.value` from U). So the writer first copies U into its own pool handle (`getReadBuf(len(U) > MaxMsgLen-HeaderLen)`, the sizing the other call sites use), parses that copy (`wire.ParseUpdateSections`), and splits U, not f(U), from the copy with the splitter `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody` already drives (`message.GetSplitter`, `Splitter.Split`, RFC 8654 Section 4), at the maximum minus g. Each chunk is serialized with `CheckedWriteTo` into a second pool handle, sized the same way and never `writeBuf`, and is written as its own write through `writeUpdateBody`: each chunk loads its own scope, computes its own cell, takes its own receipt, and carries its own U to its sent event, so `ribOut` stores each chunk's U and never a drained form. A chunk carries U's attributes (on the MP path, U's base attributes and one MP attribute chunk), so under the same scope f grows it by g at most and f(chunk) fits; a chunk that loads a different scope (a runtime scope change between two chunks) follows the same rule under its own scope and is split again only when its own f does not fit. `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody`'s rule for an error after earlier chunks (commit them with `flushWrites` before the error returns) applies unchanged, and an error a chunk's own write returns is the write's error. A body whose sections do not parse is opaque, written unchanged and unsplit, as f leaves it.
     A split refusal (redesign round 2, I-2, read at `message/update_split.go` and `chunk_mp_nlri.go` on 2026-10-14). `Splitter.Split` refuses with `ErrAttributesTooLarge` (the attributes alone leave no room, on the IPv4 path and on the MP path), with `ErrNLRITooLarge`, `ErrNLRIMalformed` or `nlrisplit.ErrUnsupported` from `ChunkMPNLRI`, wrapped as "chunking withdrawn routes", "chunking NLRI", "splitting MP_UNREACH_NLRI" or "splitting MP_REACH_NLRI", with `ErrMPOverheadTooLarge` wrapped as "splitting MP_UNREACH_NLRI" or "splitting MP_REACH_NLRI", and with a parse error wrapped as "parsing MP_UNREACH_NLRI" or "parsing MP_REACH_NLRI". It can refuse after it has emitted chunks: it emits by section, in the order IPv4 withdrawn, MP_UNREACH_NLRI, MP_REACH_NLRI, IPv4 NLRI (`splitUpdateWithMP`; `splitUpdateIPv4` emits IPv4 withdrawn, then IPv4 NLRI), computes a section's chunks before it emits the first of them, and so refuses only between sections; a refusal after the IPv4 withdrawn chunks (the IPv4 NLRI space check, or any MP error) follows withdrawals already on the wire. The writer's emit callback records the section of each chunk it wrote, so the refused section is the first section of U with no chunk recorded, and nothing of a fully emitted section is written again. The writer then takes the not-yet-emitted remainder of U (U with every fully emitted section removed: Withdrawn Routes emptied, the MP_UNREACH_NLRI or MP_REACH_NLRI attribute dropped), in a pool handle, and isolates only what f cannot fit, by the refusal (design review redesign round 3, I-2, option (a)):
       1. `ErrNLRITooLarge` (one NLRI longer than the space the section has at the maximum minus g). The splitter reports the size (`w.tooLarge`), not the offset, so the writer walks the refused section with the family's `nlrisplit.GetWithdraw` framer, the per-NLRI walker `ChunkMPNLRI` itself drives (`chunk_mp_nlri.go`, `nlrisplit.Splitter`), and picks out each NLRI longer than that space. The space is the splitter's own, never the writer's recomputation (design review redesign round 4, I-1; `ai/rules/principles.md`, declared once): `ChunkMPNLRI`'s `maxSize` is `maxSize - overhead - baseLen - 4 - highLen` (`update_split.go`, `maxMPAttrValue`, the high attributes charged twice) less the MP fixed fields and next hops for MP_REACH_NLRI, and `maxSize - overhead - baseLen` for IPv4 NLRI, and today it reaches the error only as text ("max %d", `chunk_mp_nlri.go`). So the refusal gains one typed form, declared once in package `message` beside the sentinel in `update_split.go`: `*NLRITooLargeError{Size, MaxSize int}`, whose `Unwrap` returns `ErrNLRITooLarge`, so `errors.Is(err, ErrNLRITooLarge)` still holds for every existing reader and its text is today's ("single NLRI exceeds available space: <Size> bytes, max <MaxSize>"). "Declared once" names the typed error type, not every producer of the sentinel: `BuildGroupedUnicast` (`update_build_grouped.go`) still returns a bare `ErrNLRITooLarge`, and the drain writer never reads it, because it reads only `Splitter.Split`'s refusal. It is returned at both sites that build the refusal from `w.tooLarge` today, `ChunkMPNLRI` and `SplitMPNLRI` (`chunk_mp_nlri.go`; `SplitMPNLRI` is called from `wireu/split.go`), and every `update_split.go` wrap ("chunking withdrawn routes", "chunking NLRI", "splitting MP_UNREACH_NLRI", "splitting MP_REACH_NLRI") keeps `%w`, so the writer takes `MaxSize` with `errors.As` and picks out each NLRI longer than `MaxSize`, with no arithmetic of its own. A pass that picks out no NLRI (no NLRI of the section is longer than `MaxSize`) falls to step 3, so isolation never loops. Each such NLRI goes out as its own write, with the split off and unchanged: the section's attributes and that one NLRI (an MP_REACH_NLRI carrying it alone, for an MP section), which fits the maximum because U did, and which f cannot fit because the NLRI alone exceeds the space f leaves. It carries the warning below, and its one NLRI is counted sent-untagged. The rest of the refused section without those NLRI, and every later section of the remainder, the IPv4 NLRI after MP_REACH_NLRI included, form a new U written through `writeUpdateBody` with the split on, so f applies and that U is split again at its own exact g. An oversized NLRI of a withdrawn section (IPv4 withdrawn or MP_UNREACH_NLRI) is written alone the same way; a withdrawal carries no announced route, so it is neither untagged nor counted.
       2. `ErrAttributesTooLarge` (the attributes alone exceed the maximum with f: the IPv4 path's attribute check, the MP_REACH_NLRI value space `maxMPAttrValue`, the IPv4 NLRI space after MP), and `ErrMPOverheadTooLarge` (the MP attribute's fixed fields and next hops alone exceed that space, the same fact for one MP section). No split can help that section, so the section alone (its attributes and every NLRI it carries) goes out as its own write, with the split off and unchanged, with the warning when it carries an announced NLRI (the warning rule below), every announced NLRI of it counted sent-untagged. Every other not-yet-emitted section, a withdrawn section ahead of it and every later section alike, forms a new U written with the split on, so later sections are still tried with f.
       3. Every other refusal (`ErrNLRIMalformed`, `nlrisplit.ErrUnsupported`, an MP parse error) means the splitter cannot frame the remainder, so no route in it can be isolated: the remainder is written as its own write with the split turned off. That write runs the verdict and f as every write does: when f(remainder) fits, it goes out drained; when it does not, unchanged, with the warning, its announced NLRI counted sent-untagged.
     Each new U is a strictly smaller set of NLRI than the write that produced it, because step 1 removes at least one NLRI from it (a step-1 pass that removes none goes to step 3 instead), so the re-splitting ends. Every write here is its own write through `writeUpdateBody`, with its own U on its sent event, and so its own ownership section. The warning is "bgp drain: UPDATE to peer <name> has no room for <GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN | LOCAL_PREF 0 | GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN and LOCAL_PREF 0> (<the splitter's refusal>); sent without it", one line per write sent unchanged that carries an announced NLRI; a write sent unchanged with no announced NLRI (an IPv4 withdrawn or MP_UNREACH_NLRI section alone, as when `splitUpdateWithMP` returns `ErrAttributesTooLarge` before any section because `maxMPAttrValue` is not positive with high attributes present, and the first unrecorded section is a withdrawal) logs nothing, since no route went out without the tag (design review redesign round 4, N-1), and the count is the peer's sent-untagged drain counter (count 4 below), never re-advertised. A route therefore goes out untagged only when f leaves no room for it in any message: its own NLRI exceeds the space f leaves (written alone), or its section's attributes alone exceed the maximum with f (the section written alone), or the splitter cannot frame the remainder holding it and f(remainder) does not fit. A route that fits tagged in some split is sent tagged, whatever section a neighbour's refusal came from. Refusing the write would withdraw nothing and advertise nothing, which is worse than an untagged path: RFC 8326 Section 4.2 is operator procedure ("This policy tags the paths propagated over the session with the GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN community") with no MUST, and a logged, counted untagged path states what was sent (`ai/rules/principles.md`). No reserve is taken in `fwdBucketMaxBodySize` or any rail or recovery split size, and the split runs only when this write's f does not fit, so undrained writes, untouched cells and near-maximum writes whose f adds nothing keep the full maximum. The raw path (`rawBodies`, `tryDirectWriteNoFlush`, recovery sections, all through `writeRawUpdateBody`) never reaches `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody`'s split, and `fwdBucketMerge` packs to `fwdBucketMaxBodySize`, the full maximum minus the header (`forward_bucket.go`), so a near-maximum body is the normal case for a bucketed forward during a drain; both reach this split. Every UPDATE writer reaches the site, so there is no rail list:

     | Writer | Reaches `writeUpdateBody` through | Provenance the verdict reads, under `writeMu` |
     |--------|-----------------------------------|-----------------------------------------------|
     | the forward worker's batch (`forward_pool.go`) and `tryDirectWriteNoFlush` (`forward_rs.go`): `rawBodies`, and `updates` of an item that is not `originated` | `writeRawUpdateBody`, `writeUpdateGated(update, false)` | the owner path the existing `fwdItem.writePath` yields for the body's first section, the call `allow` already makes on the owner item `Session.sentForward` (`forward_provenance.go`): `fwdItem.receivedPeer` for an item with no section provenance, the section span's source otherwise. `receivedPeer` is set today from `update.receivedPeer` in `forwardUpdateCore` (`reactor_api_forward.go`) and `forward_rs.go`, and from `src.info.peer` in `reactor_api_relay.go`, and `fwdBucketMerge` already copies it and compares it in its run condition; `recordSection` refuses a second source in one body, so the first section answers for the body. Classed by `IsIBGP()`. `allow` refuses an `adjOutForwarded` write whose owner source is nil (`errAdjOutProvenance`), so a forward never reaches provenance none. No field is added to `fwdItem` (redesign round 1, I-2). Provenance does not change with the scope, so reading it from the item judges nothing early |
     | `writeUpdateGated(update, true)`: local origination and injection, bgp-rib's peer-up, refresh, LLGR-stale and drain replays, direct through `sendUpdateCounted` or queued behind forwards | `writeOrdinaryUpdateBody`, `writeRawUpdateBody`, `writeUpdateWithoutAIGP` | the owner item `Session.sentForward` and, for a receipt (`adjOutExpected`), the destination's adj-out owner of the body's first key whose entry exists and whose `entry.revision` equals the owner revision `item.writePath` yields, keyed with `allow`'s own section walk and `adjOutWrite.key` (factored so both call one implementation; ADD-PATH path ID stripped before `fwdPathKeyFor`), read in the ownership section already open (A-18, round 5 I-1). `adjOutInitial`: `item.initialLocal` is originated, otherwise `item.receivedPeer` by its `IsIBGP()`. A nil owner item, or `originated` with `adjOutUnspecified` authority, is an origination. A source `*Peer` is classed from its settings whatever its session state (an LLGR stale source is down by definition). A receipt none of whose keys passes the test is provenance none, and `allow` refuses its NLRI; `beginAdjOut` has already reset a previous session's table, so such a write is counted in neither drain counter (round 6, N-1). The LLGR stale batch is judged here once, never in `decideStaleReadvertise` (B-2) |
     | `Session.SendAnnounce`, `sendWithdraw`, `sendRawUpdateBody`, an UPDATE through `writeMessageWithin`, an UPDATE through `SendRawMessage` in either mode (`writeOriginatedRawUpdate`) | `writeRawUpdateBody`, `writeUpdateWithoutAIGP`, `writeOriginatedRawUpdate` | owner nil: an origination. A raw body whose sections do not parse is opaque and is written unchanged |

     The destination class and mask are read from the peer's settings at the writer, never from a facts snapshot (round 3, N-3). Toward a destination whose `send-community` suppresses standard communities, the forward rails' `applyFactsSendCommunity` has already removed code 8 from U, and the verdict reads the mask itself (Q-11 (a)), so no writer adds the tag there.

     A body with no reachable NLRI is never tagged: bgp-rs's `update text`/`update hex` writes are withdrawals and End-of-RIB (`sendBatchedWithdrawals`), bgp-rr's `update text`/`update hex` writes are withdrawals and End-of-RIB too (`rr/rr.go`), and bgp-rib's lifecycle writes (`dispatchSentLifecycle`) are withdrawals only (`sentRemoval`), so the writer passes them through unchanged. Where the engine turns a departure into a replacement, the replacement goes `relayRecovery` -> `queueRecovery` -> `forwardUpdateCore(update, id, {destination}, source.info)` (`relay_recovery.go`), a forward rail with its source, so it is judged at the writer like every forward (round 3, verified; I-6; redesign 2026-10-14). Under `plan/immediate/spec-bgp-rs-replacement-on-withdrawal.md` an owner withdrawal on a non-ADD-PATH destination becomes an owed key that is not written; its round 3 (B-1) rule refuses an `adjOutExpected` receipt replay of `ribOut[destination]` while the key is owed. The drain replay is an `adjOutExpected` receipt write (I-2 keeps that authority through `queueBehindForwards`), so that rule also refuses them, which closes the hole in which a drain replay would re-advertise the dead path with GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN during the owed window (Blast Radius).
   - The withhold, at the writer. f reports its change to U as two facts, tag added and LOCAL_PREF replaced; under an undrained scope it changes nothing, so f(U, undrained) = U byte for byte. A drain-marked replay (`replay: drain`) of key K is withheld, and counted withheld, when f's change to U under the scope in force equals its change under the scope that change replaced (`replaced`, from the same scope load): the peer already holds K in that form (RFC 4271 Section 9.2), because K's last write before the change was computed under the replaced scope from the same U, and `ribOut` holds U, which no scope changes. In the stop drain the replaced scope is undrained, so a replay is withheld exactly when f changes nothing: the reflected and route-server cells, a masked eBGP destination (Q-11 (a)), and a path whose U already carries 65535:0 toward eBGP (and LOCAL_PREF 0 toward iBGP where the cell sets it). Every other replay is written. A path written live between the flag and the walk's snapshot went out drained and carries a message ID above the cut M, so the walk skips it (step 4); one written between the scope store and the moment M is read with a message ID at or below M (before its destination's take-and-release, or after that release and before M is read) is replayed once more in the same drained form, the one duplicate the design accepts (Known Limitations; redesign round 1, N-2). f adds 65535:0 only when U's COMMUNITIES lacks it, so a value the source, the export policy or `filter_community`'s egress `tag` already put in U is kept where it is and never duplicated; f is the writer's own rewrite, not a mod on the shared COMMUNITIES handler, so `filter_community`'s decided "no dedup on tag" (`TestTagExistingCommunityNoDeDup`) is untouched (the code 32 no-dedup stays the defect `plan/journal/non-idempotent-append-duplicates-the-record.md` records, RFC 8092 Section 3). `plan/spec-runtime-drain-and-undrain.md` uses this same withhold for its `undrain`-kind replays, unchanged. The marker is a value of the existing `replay` key (round 4, N-2): `replayFromMeta` (in `DispatchNLRIGroups`, which `update text` and `update hex` both reach, A-17) parses `true` or `drain` into `NLRIBatch.Replay`, which becomes a replay kind (none, refresh, drain) instead of a bool, and refuses any other value; `sendBatchUpdate` and `queueBehindForwards(batch.SentOwnerMessage, batch.Replay)` already carry `Replay` end to end, so `queueBehindForwards` writes `item.meta = {"replay": <kind>}` with no new parameter, and `sendUpdateCounted`'s `replay` argument becomes the kind it writes into `sentMeta`. Every reactor reader of the key treats `drain` as a replay: the two `sentMeta["replay"].(bool)` reads in `session_ownership.go` become kind reads. bgp-rib's readers test presence (`_, replay := event.RouteMeta[metaKeyReplay]`) and need no change, so a drain replay's sent event is skipped by `handleSent` like any replay. Provenance needs no plumbing at all (I-11, call-site table). Without the marker (a ROUTE-REFRESH answered during the drain), the write is a refresh and is re-sent as today, tagged where the verdict says so. `request bgp rib drain-replay` is hidden from help, not from dispatch (`Hidden: true`, as `request bgp rib recovery`), so an operator can run it outside a stop (round 5, N-5): when the loaded scope equals its own `replaced`, which holds only for the initial scope (epoch 0), because every change replaces the scope with a value that differs from it, the writer treats a `replay: drain` write as `refresh` (f changes nothing, no withholding, no drain counter). In this spec the scope changes once, from undrained to `router`, so the rule demotes exactly the writes the earlier wording ("with the reactor's drain flag clear") demoted; it is stated on `replaced` rather than on `router` because `plan/spec-runtime-drain-and-undrain.md` runs every undrain walk and every peer-only walk with `router` clear, and demoting those would withhold nothing and break its undrain (runtime design review round 4, ISSUE-1, amended in both specs). A `replaced` that spec records as `unconfirmed` (a change served after a failed walk) matches no scope, so a replay judged against it is neither withheld nor demoted. The writer decides it at the verdict site from its one scope load, so no `sentMeta` reassignment exists and the shared `fwdItem.meta` is never touched (round 4, I-1); `session_ownership.go` reads a replay as before, and bgp-rib's `handleSent` skips the event as it skips every replay (round 7, I-1).
   - Sent event and `ribOut` (redesign 2026-10-14). When f changed the body, the sent-event call in `writeUpdateBody` passes U's attribute block beside the wire body: the U buffer is still held there, and `notifyMessageReceiver` copies a sent message synchronously (`bytes := make(...)`, "copy bytes for async processing safety"), so the copy takes one allocation sized for both blocks, the per-message allocation it makes today, never one per route. The event carries U's attributes as a second attribute field, read by bgp-rib only; the wire body stays the event's body, so BMP's Adj-RIB-Out monitoring (`bmp_events.go`, from the sent event's raw bytes) and MRT (`OnBGPMessage`, the socket tap in `session_wire.go`) keep the wire. bgp-rib's `handleSent` and `handleSentStructured` intern U's attributes into `ribOut` when the field is present, the wire attributes otherwise (the undrained case, where they are U). Every other `ribOut` reader is fine with U, read at the producers on 2026-10-14: `handleRefresh`, `outboundResend` and `resendRoutesWithCursor` replay it through the writer, which applies f; `attachSentCommunity` (LLGR, `rib_sent_lifecycle.go`) adds LLGR_STALE to U and the writer applies f to the result; `recoveryRoutes` (`rib_recovery.go`) reads identity, source and message ID, no attribute; the adj-out signatures are the reactor's, taken from the wire. The Adj-RIB-Out show (`rib_pipeline.go` `outboundSource`) shows U, the undrained form, while drained; the stop drain answers no show after its wait in practice, and `plan/spec-runtime-drain-and-undrain.md` owns that question (its Q-8). Under its Q-8 (b) that spec adds to this sent event the provenance class the verdict read, the destination's class and mask, and a `sent-untagged` mark on a write sent unchanged under the split refusal ("sent untagged (no room)"), and `ribOut` stores the provenance class and the mark beside U, so its show renders such an entry as U (runtime spec main-thread decision, 2026-10-14); this spec adds none of them, and its f is already in the `drainverdict` leaf package that bgp-rib would import (redesign round 1, I-4, N-3). bgp-rib keeps one "snapshot taken for epoch E" state, replaced by every walk, and refuses a `request bgp rib drain-replay` whose epoch is older than E with an error naming the command, the epoch it carried and E (a reordered request); a walk of an equal or newer epoch replaces the state. The stop drain makes one scope change, so it walks once (cross-spec amendment, 2026-10-14: per epoch, so `plan/spec-runtime-drain-and-undrain.md` walks again at every scope change with no second mechanism).
   - A session that becomes Established during the drain receives its initial table from the forward rails, from the originators, and from the reactor's config-static initial sync (owner nil, so an origination at the writer). bgp-rib's peer-up replay sends nothing on a peer's first session (`collectPeerUpReplay` returns nil when `!seenBefore`) and replays `ribOut` on a re-established one (gated writer, `adjOutInitial` provenance). The verdict reaches every one of these (round 3, N-6; AC-21 covers a first session and a re-established one).
   - OSPF (v2 and v3) sets drain state that every Router-LSA origination reads beside `RouterLSAAlways`, re-originates with MaxLinkMetric, waits, replies. Any later origination during the wait (refresh, adjacency change) keeps MaxLinkMetric (I-9).
   - IS-IS sets drain state that every LSP 0 generation reads beside `cfg.Overload`, sets the overload bit, re-floods, waits, replies; a later regeneration keeps it (I-9).
   - Injected prefixes (Q-8 (b), owner 2026-10-13). The same drain state also raises the metric of every prefix the IGP itself injects, whenever a drain wait is set (`drain-on-shutdown` above 0), because MaxLinkMetric and the overload bit move transit only. RFC 2328 Section 11, Type 2 cost: "This cost has been advertised by an AS boundary router, and is the most significant part of the total path cost." An E2 external through Ze keeps winning over a MaxLinkMetric Router-LSA, and RFC 6987 Section 2 raises only "the cost of all non-stub links (links of the types other than 3)". Each producer below was read on 2026-10-13; the stored configuration and the stored prefix sets are never rewritten, only the value handed to the LSA or LSP builder while the drain state holds:
     - OSPF external metric. One engine method answers the metric an injected external carries, given its metric type (every call site below already knows the E-bit: `externalParams` sets `type2` from `r.MetricType != metricType1`, `default.go` from `di.MetricType != metricType1`): the configured or resolved metric when not draining; while draining, 0xfffffe for a type 2 external and 0x7fffff for a type 1 external. The drain steers and never withdraws (round 9, B-1/I-1): every receiver must still hold a route to the prefix through Ze while Ze is its only path, and must prefer any realistic alternate. RFC 2328 Appendix B, LSInfinity: "The metric value indicating that the destination described by an LSA is unreachable. Used in summary-LSAs and AS-external-LSAs as an alternative to premature aging (see Section 14.1). It is defined to be the 24-bit binary value of all ones: 0xffffff." Section 16.4 step (1): "If the cost specified by the LSA is LSInfinity, or if the LSA's LS age is equal to MaxAge, then examine the next LSA." 0xffffff would withdraw the route rather than steer it. The receiver's cutoff differs by type, read at Ze's receiver in `spf/external.go` `externalCandidateFrom`: a type 2 candidate's cost is the advertised metric alone (`cand.metric = rec.Metric`, RFC 2328 Section 11: the type 2 cost "is the most significant part of the total path cost"), so the distance to Ze never adds to it and 0xfffffe, LSInfinity minus one, is the largest value that stays below the `cand.metric >= LSInfinity` cutoff at every receiver. A type 1 candidate's cost is `clampMetric(baseCost, rec.Metric)` (`spf/spf.go`: LSInfinity once the sum reaches it), so 0xfffffe plus any distance of 1 or more is withdrawn; the type 1 value must leave the distance room. 0x7fffff is half the 24-bit space: a receiver keeps the route for any distance to Ze up to 0x7fffff (128 links at the 16-bit maximum cost 0xffff, far past any real area), and a type 1 alternate loses to the drained route only when its own cost exceeds 0x7fffff plus the distance to Ze, which no realistic path does. Halving splits the space evenly between the two failure modes: a value too high withdraws the route at a distant receiver, a value too low keeps it preferred over an expensive alternate. The E-bit (type 1 or 2), the External Route Tag and the forwarding address keep their values. The method is called at every engine site that hands an injected external's metric to a builder: `injectExternalLocked` (`redist_wiring.go`: OSPFv2 Type-5 through `LSDB.OriginateExternal`, Type-7 through `LSDB.OriginateNSSA`), `v6InjectExternal` (`origination_v6_external.go`: OSPFv3 AS-External through `v6OriginateExternalLSA`, NSSA through `v6OriginateNSSALSA`), `originateDefaultExternal` (`default.go`: the default Type-5 of `default-information originate` and of a redistributed default, both families, the v3 branch through `v6OriginateDefaultExternal`), and the two `originate` closures of `applyNSSADefaults` (`nssa.go`: the NSSA Type-7 default, both families, from `DefaultCost` or `externalParams`). `v6OriginateNSSADefault` (`origination_v6_nssa.go`) has test callers only (`rfc3101_origination_v6_nssa_default_test.go`) and is not a drain site (round 10, N-1). The NSSA translator's Type-5 (`nssa.go`, `db.OriginateExternal(self, tr.network, ...)` with `tr.metric`) copies another ASBR's Type-7, which Ze did not inject, and keeps its metric. Design review round 9 (N-2) confirmed that boundary at the producers, for three reasons: `translateNSSA` (`nssa.go`) skips `redist[network]`, so Ze's own Type-5 networks are never translated; an ABR's own Type-7 default carries P=0 (`propagate := !isABR` in `applyNSSADefaults`) and so is not translated; and an NSSA-only router has `canType5` false, so `externalScope` reports it as no translator
     - OSPF re-origination of the externals at drain entry: the OSPF drain handler sets the drain state, then runs `originateSelfLSAs` (Router-LSA and, for v3, Intra-Area-Prefix-LSAs) and `reconcileExternalImports` (`redist_wiring.go`), which replays every remembered import through `injectExternalLocked` and then runs `applyDefaultInformation` and `applyNSSADefaults`. Every later origination in the wait (a route redistributed during the drain, an interface change that calls `reconcileExternalImports` from `instance.go`) reads the same drain state, so the raised metric survives it (R-11)
     - OSPFv2 stub links. `lsdb.OriginInput` holds one `MaxMetric` boolean today, set from `RouterLSAAlways` through `OriginateFromTopology(router, maxMetric)` (`instance.go`); the link list is built in `routerLinks(in OriginInput)` (`lsdb/origination.go`, called by `OriginateRouter`), which applies it to non-stub links only, and that is the edit site (round 9, N-5). The input gains a second boolean, `MaxMetricStub`, set only by the drain state, and both `OriginateFromTopology` and `v6OriginateSelf` take the pair in place of the one boolean. With it set, every type 3 link `routerLinks` appends, the interface subnet stub (metric from the interface cost; broadcast, NBMA and point-to-point interfaces) and the 255.255.255.255 host stub of a point-to-multipoint interface address (metric 0; `routerLinks` emits it only in its `isPtMP` branch, and a point-to-point interface emits no host stub, round 9 I-3), is originated at MaxLinkMetric 0xffff, the widest value the 16-bit Router-LSA metric holds. `always` alone keeps RFC 6987's stub treatment: stub links keep their cost
     - OSPFv3 stub prefixes. OSPFv3 Router-LSAs carry no stub links; the router's own prefixes ride its Router-referencing Intra-Area-Prefix-LSA, built in `v6OriginateSelf` from `v6InterfacePrefixes` (`origination_v6.go`, metric from `iface.Cost`). With `MaxMetricStub` set, each of those prefixes is originated at 0xffff, the widest value of the 16-bit PrefixMetric. The Network-referencing Intra-Area-Prefix-LSA (`v6OriginateNetworkIntraAreaPrefix`) keeps its metrics: its prefixes are reached through the transit network, whose cost through Ze is already MaxLinkMetric
     - IS-IS IP reachability. `levelState` (`lsdb_wiring.go`) assembles each level's `state.Prefixes` from `e.prefixes` (connected), `e.redistPrefixes` (redistributed) and `e.leakedPrefixes` (inter-level leak), and `state.PrefixesV6` from their IPv6 counterparts. While draining, `levelState` sets the metric of each entry in that assembled copy: a wide entry (TLV 135, TLV 236) takes 0x7f000000, half of MAX_PATH_METRIC 0xfe000000, and a narrow entry (`PrefixInfo.Narrow`, which `fragmentTLVs` in `lsdb/origination.go` keeps in TLV 128 or 130) takes 63. RFC 5305 Section 4: "If a prefix is advertised with a metric larger then MAX_PATH_METRIC (0xFE000000, see paragraph 3.0), this prefix MUST NOT be considered during the normal SPF computation." RFC 5308 Section 2: "Just as in [RFC5305], if a prefix is advertised with a metric larger than MAX_V6_PATH_METRIC (0xFE000000), this prefix MUST not be considered during the normal Shortest Path First (SPF) computation." That quote bounds the prefix's own value, not the path sum, and the sum is what a receiver cuts on (round 9, B-1). Ze's receiver, `spf.BuildRoutes` (`spf/route.go`), computes `clampMetric(nr.Metric, p.Metric)` and skips the prefix when the total is `>= MaxPathMetric`; `clampMetric` (`spf/spf.go`) returns `MaxPathMetric` for any sum above it; the IPv6 builder (`spf/ipv6.go`) does the same after its `> MaxV6PathMetric` check, and the leak path (`spf/leak.go`) cuts the same way. So a prefix at 0xfe000000 is withdrawn at every receiver at distance 1 or more, and Ze's loopback, a connected prefix in `e.prefixes`, would become unreachable for the whole drain, dropping iBGP sessions to it and every BGP next hop resolved through it in the middle of the RFC 8326 wait. 0x7f000000 leaves the other half of the space for the distance: a receiver keeps the route for any distance to Ze below 0x7f000000 (127 links at the largest usable link metric 0xfffffe, RFC 5305 Section 3), and an alternate loses to the drained prefix only when its own total exceeds 0x7f000000 plus the distance to Ze, which no realistic path does. The same halving as the OSPF type 1 value, for the same two failure modes. Any future change to this value MUST keep distance plus value below MAX_PATH_METRIC for every realistic distance, or the loopback is withdrawn again. RFC 1195 Section 3.2: "If this sum results in a metric value greater than 63 (the maximum value that can be reported in level 2 LSPs), then the value 63 must be used." 63 is the largest value the 6-bit narrow field holds, and Ze's receiver applies no narrow-specific path cutoff (the narrow total goes through the same `clampMetric` against 0xfe000000), so 63 is the least preferred narrow value and stays routable at Ze. A receiver that applies RFC 1195's narrow MaxPathMetric (1023, RFC 5308 Section 2: "MAX_PATH_METRIC (1023) from the original specification in [RFC1195]") keeps it for any distance up to 960, 15 links at the narrow maximum 63. That bound holds for FRR in `metric-style narrow` (`process_N`: `dist > MAX_NARROW_PATH_METRIC`); in `metric-style transition`, the interop receiver's style, FRR applies the wide bound (`dist > MAX_WIDE_PATH_METRIC`) instead (round 10, N-4). `originationUnchanged` compares the assembled state, so the raised metrics make the drain's regeneration a change and it is flooded; a later regeneration in the wait assembles through `levelState` again and keeps them (R-11)
     - Kind `restart`: OSPF with the restarter role raises none of these (it originates no MaxLinkMetric either, AC-25 (a)); OSPF without it and IS-IS drain on `restart` as on `stop`, injected prefixes included (AC-25 (b), (c))
   - On kind `restart` the IGPs decide for their own protocol (I-2, round 2 I-5): OSPF with the restarter role enabled (`restarterEnabled()`, `config.go`) calls `prepareRestart` (`gr_restarter.go`) with the grace reason the drain RPC carries on each engine: the v2 engine and every running OSPFv3 address-family engine (`v6EngineSet`), each with its own `grManager` and its own wait set; OSPF replies when every engine's wait has ended (round 5, N-8). `prepareRestart` originates one Grace-LSA per interface and records the restart fact, originating no MaxLinkMetric. Each engine's wait set is a set of (interface, router ID) pairs, because Grace-LSAs are per interface while the restart fact's `Expected` holds router IDs only: for each interface `grOriginateGraceLSAs` walks, the neighbours Full on that interface (`e.neighbors.FloodNeighbors(iface)`, state `NeighborStateFull`) to which this origination's Grace-LSA was queued (round 5, I-7). "Queued" is recorded where it happens and is never inferred by a later query (round 7, I-2, I-3), in three steps:
     1. `grOriginateGraceLSAs` returns one result per interface it walks (every non-passive named interface of `lsdbTopology()`, refused ones included), holding the interface name and either the Grace-LSA instance it installed (the header `OriginateLinkSelf` returns with `ok` true) or refused. The v2 branch stops discarding `OriginateOpaque`'s result, and `v6OriginateGraceLSA` (`gr_preserve.go`) returns the header beside its `ok`, as `OriginateOpaque` does, instead of the v3 branch dropping a refused interface with `continue`. `prepareRestart` returns that result to its callers with its error: `grPrepare` does not read it, `drain.go` builds the wait set from it. The `graceLSAs` "originated" metric counts the results that hold an instance, so on v2 it stops counting a refused origination as originated.
     2. `floodLink` (`lsdb/link_scope.go`), when `queueRetransmit` returns true for a self-originated Grace key (`isGraceLSA(key)` and `key.AdvertisingRouter == d.selfRouter`, the router ID read under `d.mu`), records under `d.mu` the instance it queued for that neighbour key, in one new LSDB map beside `retransmit` (`lsdb/lsdb.go`): neighbour key, then Grace key, to the queued LSA header. The self test is needed because `floodLink` also floods Grace-LSAs this router did not originate (round 8, I-1): `LSDB.Tick` (`lsdb/aging.go`) marks every link-store entry that reaches MaxAge as purged, whoever originated it, and calls `floodLink` for it, so a neighbour's Grace-LSA held here as helper and never flushed by that neighbour (it crashed mid-restart, or failed its exit) is flooded under the other router's Grace key, and `isGraceLSA` reads only the type and opaque type, never the advertising router. An entry is replaced by the next flood of that key to that neighbour and is never deleted by an acknowledgement, an interface teardown (`ReleaseLink` deletes `retransmit` lists and the per-interface stores, not this map, so the map outlives it) or `removeFromAllRetransmit`, so a pair that acknowledged, or lost its adjacency, before the wait set is built still reads as queued. The map is bounded by the neighbour keys flood-eligible at some self Grace flood times the self Grace keys flooded on their interface: on v2 one Grace key per neighbour key (the Type-9 opaque type 3 key, the same on every interface), on v3 one per interface ID the interface has carried, each under every router ID this process has held. A self Grace key is flooded by the restart paths and by its own MaxAge flush, and each flood replaces the entry, so the map never holds more than one header per neighbour key and Grace key. A new exported query, `LSDB.GraceQueued(nbr NeighborKey, key types.LSAKey) (packet.LSAHeader, bool)` in `lsdb/flooding.go`, reads it under `d.mu`.
     3. Once `prepareRestart` returns, a Full pair joins the wait set only when its interface's result holds an instance and `GraceQueued` holds that same instance for it (`sameInstance`, `lsdb/flooding.go`). Every other Full pair is logged "ospf drain: Grace-LSA not queued for neighbour" with the interface and router ID, and is never counted acknowledged: each Full neighbour of a refused interface, and each Full neighbour with no matching record.

     "Not in the set" therefore means not queued by this origination, and "in the set and no longer pending" means acknowledged or lost, decided below. The producers queue nothing for a Full neighbour in these cases: `OriginateLinkSelf` installs and floods nothing, and returns `ok` false, when the existing self Grace-LSA has the same body (`existingLinkSelfBodyUnchanged`) or `nextLinkOwnSequence` refuses inside MinLSInterval, on v2 through `OriginateOpaque` and on v3 through `v6OriginateGraceLSA`, so the interface's result is refused; v2 `floodLink` skips a neighbour that is not opaque-capable for the Type-9 Grace-LSA (RFC 5250 Section 3.1); `queueRetransmit` returns false once the neighbour's list holds `MaxRetransmitPerNeighbor` entries; `floodLink` returns before its loop when the interface is missing from its topology snapshot or the entry is gone; and a neighbour that was not flood-eligible at the flood and reached Full afterwards gets the Grace-LSA the link LSDB holds through database exchange, with nothing queued. Each would otherwise read as acknowledged at once, the false acknowledgement RFC 3623 Section 2.1 (below) makes the wait exist for. Rejected, round 6's query of the retransmission lists after `grOriginateGraceLSAs` returns: `floodLink` sends the LSU in the same call, the LSAck is handled on the receive goroutine (`clearRetransmit`, under `d.mu`), and the origination goes on to the next interface, so a neighbour that acknowledged inside that window read as not pending and was logged "not queued", a false operator line. Also rejected, returning the queued neighbour keys up through `floodLink`, `installLinkOriginated` and `OriginateLinkSelf`: every Link-LSA origination takes that path, so it changes three shared signatures and builds a slice per flood, where the map is written only for a Grace key. A pair is acknowledged when a new exported LSDB query, `LSDB.RetransmitPending(nbr NeighborKey, key types.LSAKey) bool` in `lsdb/flooding.go`, read under `d.mu` beside the unexported `queueRetransmit` and `clearRetransmit`, answers that the interface's Grace-LSA is no longer on that neighbour key's retransmission list, and the neighbour is still Full on that interface (`hasFullNeighbor(iface, router)`). The Grace-LSA key per interface is the key of the instance the interface's result holds (the header's own key, which `installLinkLocked` stores under and `queueRetransmit` keys by), so the wait derives no key itself, on either version: the v2 key helper round 6 (N-2) planned, and the wait's reuse of v3's `v6SummaryLSID(InterfaceID)`, are dropped (round 7, I-2), and a test pins the result's key to the key `OriginateOpaque` installs on v2 and `v6OriginateGraceLSA` installs on v3. Absence from the list alone is not acknowledgement: an interface teardown deletes the lists of every neighbour on it (`lsdb/link_scope.go`), and `removeFromAllRetransmit` drops a key from every list, so a neighbour that leaves Full before its list empties is logged "ospf drain: adjacency lost before Grace-LSA acknowledgement" with the interface and router ID, and is not counted acknowledged. A neighbour that leaves Full and is Full again by the next check has had its list deleted at teardown, so it reads as not pending and Full and is counted acknowledged without an LSAck. The outcome holds, because the new adjacency's database exchange gives it the Grace-LSA the link LSDB still holds, so "acknowledged" in the drain's figures means acknowledged or resynchronised (round 7, N-2). A third route empties a list with no LSAck and no teardown (round 8, N-3): a Newer receipt of a link LSA calls `removeFromAllRetransmit(area, key)` (`ReceiveUpdate`'s LinkLocal branch, `lsdb/flooding.go`), and while `selfFlushSuppressed()` holds, which it does from the moment `prepareRestart` raises `gracefulStop` (`instance.go`, `SetSelfFlushSuppress`), `handleSelfLinkReceived` (`lsdb/link_scope.go`) returns false, so a neighbour's newer copy of our own Grace key is installed and the key leaves every list. Every Full neighbour on that interface then reads as acknowledged without an LSAck. The case needs a neighbour holding a higher sequence of our Grace key than this origination, which is rare once `deletePurgedLinkIfAcked` has reset `linkOwn`, and in it the neighbours hold a Grace-LSA under our key, though its body is the one the newer copy carries and not this origination's. The spec states the case and does not close it. Rejected, counting acknowledged only on a recorded `clearRetransmit` of the matching instance: the round-7 Grace map holds only the queued header, so marking an acknowledgement adds a field to its value and a new query in place of `RetransmitPending`, and it would also stop counting the resynchronised neighbour above, whose list is deleted at teardown with no `clearRetransmit`, so that pair would wait for the deadline. The wait ends when every pair is acknowledged or lost, or when the drain context ends, which logs each unacknowledged pair by interface and router ID (round 4, I-7; round 5, I-7). OSPFv3 has no LSDB of its own, so the one query serves both versions. Replying once the Grace-LSAs are queued is not enough: the reply lets teardown close OSPF's sockets at once, so a Grace-LSA dropped or not yet sent is never retransmitted. RFC 3623 Section 2.1: "If Router X wants to ensure that its neighbors receive the grace-LSAs, it should retransmit the grace-LSAs until they are acknowledged (i.e., perform standard OSPF reliable flooding of the grace-LSAs). If one or more fully adjacent neighbors do not receive grace-LSAs, they will more than likely cause premature termination of the graceful restart procedure (see Section 4)." The grace reason is chosen per entry point (round 4, I-8). The drain RPC carries, beside kind `restart`, a restart cause with two values the hub knows without naming a protocol: `in-place` (SSH `restart`) and `reboot` (`request reboot` and SSH `reboot`, the two that set `rebootRequested`). OSPF maps `in-place` to 1 (software restart) and `reboot` to 2 (software reload/upgrade), the value `request ospf graceful-restart` already uses (`grPrepare`, `grReasonReload`). RFC 3623 Appendix A: "Encodes the reason for the router restart as one of the following: 0 (unknown), 1 (software restart), 2 (software reload/upgrade) or 3 (switch to redundant control processor)." The value matters to a helper: RFC 3623 Section 3.1 lists as example policies "a) never act as helper, b) never allow the grace period to exceed a Time T, c) only help on software reloads/upgrades, or d) never act as a helper for specific routers (specified by OSPF Router ID)", so a helper under policy (c) refuses reason 1, which is why the reboot entry points do not send it; answering at once is not enough, because nothing but `prepareRestart` starts a planned restart, and its only caller today is the operator command `request ospf graceful-restart` (`grPrepare`), so without the call the adjacencies drop with no Grace-LSA and the neighbours flush Ze (round 3, I-4). OSPF with the helper role only, or with no graceful restart, drains as on `stop`; IS-IS, which has no graceful restart in Ze, drains as on `stop`. BGP is already stopped in silence on this kind (`stopForRestart`), so an IGP wait spends from the BGP peers' restart time (R-12).
6. Hub continues the existing teardown: gNMI, API shutdowns, `apiServer.Stop()` (BGP Cease), then `eng.Stop` with its own context.

**Signal state table** (I-4). "Signal" means an OS SIGINT or SIGTERM delivered after `waitLoop` returned; the value `request shutdown` injects into `stopCh` is not one, so after `request shutdown` the operator's first OS signal is the one counted. One goroutine owns `forceCh` from `waitLoop`'s return to exit and reads the phase the hub publishes.

| Phase when the signal arrives | Drain not yet finished | Drain finished, skipped, or aborted |
|-------------------------------|------------------------|--------------------------------------|
| Fence (reload worker, confirm window, `commitReloads.close()`) | the drain is marked skipped (log "drain skipped by signal"); the fence continues | force exit 1, as today |
| Drain | the drain context is cancelled (log "drain aborted by signal"); teardown continues, BGP still sends Cease | not reachable |
| Teardown | not reachable | force exit 1, as today |

The line printed on entering each phase states what the next signal does (round 2, N-6; `ai/rules/user-facing-errors.md`):

| Phase entered | Printed line |
|---------------|--------------|
| Fence | "Stopping: finishing config changes (Ctrl+C again to skip the drain)..." |
| Drain, with at least one drain-registered plugin | "Draining routing protocols for up to N s (Ctrl+C again to stop now)..." |
| Teardown, or a drain skipped or aborted | "Shutting down (Ctrl+C again to force)..." |

### Boundaries Crossed
| Boundary | How | Verified |
|----------|-----|----------|
| Hub -> plugin server | new server method fanning the drain out: it queues one request to each live generation's long-lived drain caller and reads the reply channels under the drain deadline (Data Flow step 4) | No (design) |
| Plugin server -> drain caller | the caller set (keyed by process, under a server mutex), a request channel per caller, a capacity-1 reply channel per request; `superviseProcess` starts and ends each caller with its generation | No (design); `superviseProcess` runs one generation's whole life (`server/failure_policy.go`, read 2026-10-14) |
| Engine <-> plugin | new `drain` RPC on the plugin connection (DirectBridge in-process, socket external), `rpc.DrainInput`/`DrainOutput` value types | No (design) |
| BGP plugin -> reactor | the reactor's drain scope, loaded once per write by `Session.writeUpdateBody` inside `writeMu`, where the one drain verdict and its rewrite run before `ownership.filter`, reading the write's owner item, its `writePath` owner and the adj-out owner (redesign 2026-10-14) | No (design) |
| Reactor -> bgp-rib, ordering | per-peer `writeMu` snapshot, then `DrainEventsApplied` (a `Barrier` delivery through bgp-rib's `Process.eventChan`), then the dispatch (B-2) | No (design); the barrier itself is in use by `relay_recovery.go` |
| BGP plugin -> bgp-rib | `request bgp rib drain-replay` through the inter-plugin command dispatch; bgp-rib's replay commands carry `replay: drain`, parsed by `replayFromMeta` into the `NLRIBatch.Replay` kind and carried through `sendBatchUpdate` and `queueBehindForwards` as `Replay` already is (round 4, N-2, I-9). No provenance crosses this boundary (round 4, I-11) | No (design) |
| Session writer -> bgp-rib | the sent event of a write f changed carries U's attribute block beside the wire body, from the sent-event call in `Session.writeUpdateBody`; bgp-rib's `ribOut` interns U (redesign 2026-10-14) | No (design) |
| SDK event loop -> drain worker | a channel to one long-lived drain worker per connection, which answers the drain request's ID itself | No (design) |
| Daemon -> external plugin process | spawned in its own process group | No (design) |
| Daemon -> systemd | unit `TimeoutStopSec`, `KillMode=mixed` | No (design) |
| Daemon -> gokrazy supervisor | SIGTERM to ze's process group, 100 s, then SIGKILL | Yes (pinned module source, A-5) |

### Integration Points
- `sdk.Registration` gains `DrainBudget`; `pkg/plugin/sdk` gains `OnDrain` beside `OnBye`.
- `internal/component/plugin/server/startup.go` validates the budget like `validateDoctorCheckDecls`.
- hub stop path in `cmd/ze/hub/main.go`; lifecycle handlers in `internal/component/plugin/server/system.go`; `cmd/ze/hub/service_ssh.go`; stop kind in `cmd/ze/hub/infra_setup.go`.
- BGP: `internal/component/bgp/plugin/register.go` (handler); the reactor's drain scope (`internal/component/bgp/reactor/drain.go`) and the drain verdict with its rewrite f, declared in a leaf package `internal/component/bgp/drainverdict/` that imports nothing from the reactor or bgp-rib (redesign round 1, N-3: built there from the start, so `plan/spec-runtime-drain-and-undrain.md` Q-8 (b) can import it from bgp-rib with no move; neither spec is implemented, the same reasoning as runtime round 1 I-1), called at one site, `Session.writeUpdateBody` (`session_write.go`), before `ownership.filter`, with the near-maximum split before `beginAdjOut`; the forward provenance read from the existing `fwdItem.writePath` owner, no rail edit; the ordering sequence (per-peer `writeMu` take-and-release, the message-ID cut, `DrainEventsApplied`, dispatch); U's attribute block on the sent event and bgp-rib's `ribOut` storing it; the `replay` kind through `replayFromMeta`, `NLRIBatch.Replay`, `sendBatchUpdate`, `queueBehindForwards`, `sendUpdateCounted`, and the bool carriers in `peer_send.go`, `peer.go` and `peer_initial_sync.go` (round 4, N-2; round 5, N-3); the re-advertised and withheld counts in `writeUpdateBody`, both from the verdict's one scope load, and the before/after delta of the rs spec's per-peer owed-refusal counter on `Peer` (round 5, I-5, I-6); bgp-rib's hidden `request bgp rib drain-replay` command running the `outboundResend` walk under the `peerMu` write lock, skipping entries above the cut. In `session_ownership.go` the two `replay` reads change from bool to kind, and the section walk and `adjOutWrite.key` are factored so the verdict's provenance lookup shares them (round 5, I-1); nothing in `adj_rib_out.go` changes (round 2, B-1). No drain step exists in `r.orderedEgressSteps`, the route-server rail or `exportFilterForBody` (redesign 2026-10-14).
- OSPF/IS-IS: their `register.go` handlers; drain state read by OSPFv2 `OriginateFromTopology`, OSPFv3 `v6OriginateSelf`, and IS-IS LSP generation in `lsdb_wiring.go`, and reported by `show ospf` (`stubRouterView.Active`, v2), by Ze's own Router-LSA in `show ospf ipv6 database router detail` (v3, round 5 I-8), and IS-IS LSP 0's `overload` in its show view; on kind `restart` OSPF's handler tests `restarterEnabled()` and calls `prepareRestart` on each engine with the grace reason mapped from the restart cause, then waits for the Grace-LSA acknowledgements per (interface, router ID) pair through the new exported `LSDB.RetransmitPending`, or the deadline (round 4, N-6, I-7, I-8; round 5, I-7, N-8).
- `internal/core/redistevents/registry.go`: each registration declares `drained by <plugin>` or `no drain: <reason>`; a repeated name with a different disposition panics.
- `internal/component/plugin/process/process.go`: external plugins in their own process group.
- systemd plugin unit generation and its doctor check.

### Architectural Verification
| Check | Holds? | Evidence |
|-------|--------|----------|
| No bypassed layers (data flows through the intended path) | Yes (design) | drain goes hub -> plugin server -> RPC -> plugin; lifecycle handlers stop calling the reactor directly |
| No unintended coupling (components stay isolated) | Yes (design) | hub and server know only "plugins with a drain handler" |
| No duplicated functionality (extends existing, does not recreate) | Yes (design) | callback pattern of `OnBye`/`OnDoctorCheck`; Stage 1 validation pattern of `validateDoctorCheckDecls`; the BGP tag is one rewrite at the single writer every UPDATE reaches, `Session.writeUpdateBody`, modeled on the existing AIGP strip (`writeUpdateWithoutAIGP`, `stripAIGPAttributes`), with no step in either egress chain or `exportFilterForBody`; the near-maximum split reuses the existing `message.Splitter`; re-advertisement reuses bgp-rib's existing `outboundResend` walk over `ribOut` |
| Zero-copy preserved where applicable (refs, not copies) | Yes (design) | re-advertisement replays `ribOut`'s U through the existing writer; f copies U once into a pool buffer and writes f(U) back into `writeBuf`, as the AIGP strip does, with no per-route allocation and no attribute-mods path; the near-maximum split serializes each chunk into a pool buffer |
| Cost on the non-draining hot path | Yes (design) | one atomic load of the drain scope per UPDATE write, in `Session.writeUpdateBody`, inside the `writeMu` section it already holds; no step is added to either egress chain; the adj-out lookup for a receipt's provenance and the near-maximum size test run only while draining; `fwdItem` gains no field. No allocation, no lock. While drained, a changed body costs one pool buffer and two copies (the AIGP strip pattern) and the sent event's one copy grows by U's attribute block (redesign 2026-10-14) |
| Registration over hardcoding, outbound | Yes (design) | plugins register `OnDrain` + `DrainBudget`; no protocol name in hub or server |
| Registration over hardcoding, inbound | Yes (design) | searched: hub stop path, `system.go` lifecycle handlers, `startup.go` validators, `process/manager.go`; none learns a protocol name; the ceiling is declared once in `pkg/plugin/rpc` and the unit generator derives `TimeoutStopSec` from it |

## Risks & Assumptions

### Assumptions
| ID | Assumption | Basis (file/doc/user statement) | If wrong | Validated by | Status |
|----|-----------|--------------------------------|----------|--------------|--------|
| A-1 | Routing `request shutdown` and SSH `stop` through the hub still yields a Cease to every peer | the BGP engine run in `bgp/plugin/register.go` calls `bgpReactor.Stop()` when `p.Run` returns on connection close | peers would see a bare FIN on `request shutdown` | functional test asserting NOTIFICATION 6/2 after `request shutdown` (AC-8) | unvalidated |
| A-2 | FRR bgpd lowers preference of a path carrying GRACEFUL_SHUTDOWN without extra config, or the interop config applies an inbound route-map that does | FRR `bgp graceful-shutdown` doc (research §1.2) covers the sender; receiver handling not read in FRR source | interop must configure the receiver side explicitly | read FRR `bgp_route.c` gshut handling before writing the scenario | unvalidated |
| A-3 | The plugin connections and the reactor are fully live between `commitReloads.close()` and `apiServer.Stop()` | research §1.5; code read of the hub stop path | drain RPC cannot be delivered | wiring test AC-1 | unvalidated |
| A-4 | `KillMode=mixed` delivers SIGTERM to the main PID only and SIGKILL to the whole cgroup at timeout | systemd.kill(5): with `mixed` "the SIGTERM signal is sent to the main process while the subsequent SIGKILL signal is sent to all remaining processes of the unit's control group" (quoted from systemd v257 `man/systemd.kill.xml`, the version the validation ran under; the Debian slim image ships no man pages) | external plugins die at drain start under systemd | unit test reading the generated unit (AC-15), plus one `systemd-run --user --property=KillMode=mixed` stop of ze with an external drain plugin on the dev host, output pasted here (a `.ci` cannot tell kill modes apart, I-10) | validated (mixed signalling) 2026-10-15 (phase 3); timeout-kill of stray members by documentation. The unit half is green (AC-15). The stop ran under systemd 257 (257.13-1~deb13u1) as PID 1 in a privileged Debian trixie container, with `ze` and `le` cross-built for linux/arm64 from a `git archive` of 229d6a4218, `systemd-run --property=Type=simple --property=KillMode=<mode> --property=TimeoutStopSec=90`, an external plugin `run "le test fixture plugin/drain-responder"` (holds the drain 1 s), and `strace -f -e trace=none -e signal=SIGTERM,SIGKILL,SIGINT` on the plugin. Green, `KillMode=mixed` (ze pid 316 pgid 316, plugin pid 332 pgid 331): the plugin's strace holds no SIGTERM, and the journal reads "02:42:19.467343 systemd[1]: Stopping ze-a4-mixed3.service", "02:42:19.468912 ze[316]: drain-fixture: drain received kind=stop deadline-in=5s", "02:42:20.476114 ze[316]: drain-fixture: drain answered", "02:42:20.480023 ze[316]: Ze stopped.", "02:42:20.485388 systemd[1]: ze-a4-mixed3.service: Main process exited, code=exited, status=0/SUCCESS"; the plugin's threads end "02:42:20.476827 +++ killed by SIGKILL +++", after the answer, from ze's own teardown of its plugin group, so systemd had nothing left to kill. Red, `KillMode=control-group` (systemd's default, plugin pid 286): "296 02:42:12.410120 --- SIGTERM {si_signo=SIGTERM, si_code=SI_USER, si_pid=1, si_uid=0} ---" reaches the plugin in the same millisecond as ze and the drain is cut: "drain failed ... error=\"send request: set write deadline: ... use of closed network connection\"", "drain-fixture: FAIL connection closed before the drain was answered", "ZE-OBSERVER-FAIL: drain fixture: stopped with no drain answered", and no "drain received" line. Timeout half, `KillMode=mixed` with `TimeoutStopSec=2` and `plugin/drain-never-replies`: ze does not read `TimeoutStopSec` and does not adapt its drain to a shorter one. Its deadline is `stopDrainDeadline` (`cmd/ze/hub/drain.go`), the earlier of the 60 s ceiling and the plugin's declared budget, and the 2 s in "Draining routing protocols for up to 2 s" and "drain deadline passed with no answer ... elapsed=2.01s" is `drainNeverRepliesBudget` (`internal/test/fixture/register_drain.go`), which matched the unit's 2 s by chance. A shorter `TimeoutStopSec` is what `ze doctor` warns about (`doctor-service-stop`). systemd then logged "State 'stop-sigterm' timed out. Killing." and "Killing process 369 (ze) with signal SIGKILL." for each remaining member of the cgroup. The plugin had already been SIGKILLed by ze's teardown 4 ms earlier (02:42:38.124134), so systemd's timeout SIGKILL on a non-main cgroup member was NOT observed: that half rests on the systemd.kill(5) text quoted above. Unit and doctor under real systemd (2026-10-15, phase 3 review): the same image with `dbus` installed, `ze` cross-built for linux/arm64 with the full feature tags from the working tree, `ze init`, then `ze install systemd` ("Created symlink '/etc/systemd/system/multi-user.target.wants/ze.service' → '/etc/systemd/system/ze.service'.", "service installed and enabled"), then `systemctl daemon-reload`. `systemd-analyze verify /etc/systemd/system/ze.service` exits 0 once `procps` supplies `/bin/kill`; without it: "ze.service: Command /bin/kill is not executable: No such file or directory". `systemctl show -p TimeoutStopUSec -p KillMode ze.service` printed "TimeoutStopUSec=1min 30s" and "KillMode=mixed", and `ze doctor`, run as the `ze` user with `ze.test.doctor.platform=systemd` (the container is otherwise detected as a container and the check skipped), printed no `doctor-service-stop` line: "ready (0 errors, 8 warnings)". With a drop-in of `TimeoutStopSec=89.999s` and `KillMode=control-group`, systemctl printed "TimeoutStopUSec=1min 29.999000s" and "KillMode=control-group", and doctor printed "WARN  [doctor-service-stop] systemd kills ze.service 1min 29.999000s after the stop signal, below the 90 s ze needs to drain (60 s) and tear down (30 s); run `sudo ze install systemd --force` and `sudo systemctl daemon-reload`, and remove any drop-in `systemctl cat ze.service` shows overriding it" and "WARN  [doctor-service-stop] systemd stops ze.service with KillMode=control-group, which sends the stop SIGTERM to every external plugin before ze can drain it; run `sudo ze install systemd --force` ...". Other spans systemd 257 printed for `TimeoutStopSec=`: 2s "2s", 500ms "500ms", 3month "3month", 1y "1y", infinity "infinity", 1h30min "1h 30min", 90061.5s "1d 1h 1min 1.500000s"; each is a case of `TestParseSystemdTimespan`. Before `dbus` was installed the non-root query failed with "Failed to connect to system scope bus via local transport: No such file or directory", which doctor's warning now carries |

Left: `ipc/rpc.go` `PluginConn` falls back to `pc.Conn` when `pc.mux == nil` | the drain worker must use a separate drain-done message instead of the request's answer | read the `pc.Conn` fallback's `CallRPC` before Phase 1 | confirmed for the mux and the bridge (round 2, N-1); the `pc.Conn` fallback is unvalidated |

| A-13 | bgp-rib's `ribOut` holds every path currently advertised to each up peer, forwarded and originated, with the attributes sent and its provenance (`SourcePeer`, `LocalOrigin`), and the drain walk replays every entry of it. The drain walk includes both: it skips no entry for eligibility, because the path is already on the peer and the drain only re-tags it (round 3, N-7) | `handleSent` records every sent UPDATE except replay and rib-lifecycle events (`rib.go`) | a path missing from `ribOut`, or filtered out of the walk, is never re-advertised with the tag | unit test comparing `ribOut`'s keys with the reactor's `adjRIBOut` keys per peer after a mixed forward and originate run; `TestRIBDrainWalkIncludesIneligible` (RPKI-refused and ConfigStatic entries replayed) | unvalidated |
